KULM DIAMOND JUBILEE

KULM, NORTH DAKOTA   JUNE 26 & 27, 1967
SUPPLEMENT TO 1957 JUBILEE BOOK

Dedication. . .

This book supplement to the 65th Kulm Anniversary book, is dedicated to those
who remained, and those who came, to live and die on North Dakota soil.  To
those, both in town and on the farms that resisted the temptation to migrate
during the many times when adversity, both economic and natural, make other
pastures shine with green brilliance and promise.  And, to those that joined
them here at Kulm and in North Dakota to pick up the thread of history where
others left off.  And, to those who have moved to other parts of the world, but
remain with us in heart.

This humble effort is done on their behalf.


Forward. . :

Planners for this Kulm 75th Anniversary found themselves in a rather unique
position of planning a major historical observance just a few months less than
ten years since a similar occasion.  In most of the general committee men's
minds, the 65th Jubilee still held vivid memories, and in some there was
anticipation caused by their absence ten years ago.

In the minds of all was the enthusiasm this great occasion generated.  The
feeling of those that started plans for this Anniversary observance was that it
should be as great an occasion as possible, and joined by others, have worked
toward that end.

The Jubilee committee felt that a publication of some sort was desirable for
this observance, but that the work and expense of an entirely new history more
or less duplicating the 1957 book was unjustified.

In the absence of other efforts, this writer offered the publication of a
supplement to the 1957 Jubilee book, not to duplicate but to bring up to date,
and offer a different approach to Kulm's 75 years of history.  With the
assistance of the staff of the newspaper, and many other helpers and
contributors, the following information was compiled from back copies of The
Kulm Messenger, Alumni Ass'n. files, school files, and from the hundreds of
family albums and memories of residents, city and farm alike.  Many contributed
pictures, and many new pictures were taken.  Many were taken directly from the
files of the newspaper.

The book depends on financing only from itself without sponsorship, and is
completely a local product, including printing and binding.

We hope that the product meets with your approval and many hours of enjoyment
will be received during its reading.

--John E. Peters, Publisher
--The Kulm Messenger


WHY KULM?

In a great country where the names of cities and towns often time are duplicated
dozens of times over the 50 States, we find ourselves living in and near a city
with a rather unique name, unduplicated in the United States, but with many
counterparts in the world.  Kulm is a blending of old and new.  Its roots are in
Europe and Asia Minor, yet several nationalities blended to make Kulm what it is
today.

The early settlers came here from various places for free land, freedom of
worship, to get out from under the heavy hand of the governments from which they
came, for pure wanderlust, and to take part in a new land.

When the Northern Pacific Railroad was given a land grant to push a railroad
from the twin cities to the Pacific Coast, the recruiters pushed throughout
Europe to entice farm people and businessmen to come to the Midwestern and
western United Stated to settle, to build towns and commerce and thereby create
business for the railroad.  To this day, real estate transfers and deeds make
reference to the Northern Pacific Railroad in a wide belt across the northern
tier of States.

The lure of free land brought many nationalities to the Dakotas, among them the
Swedish and German peoples that make up the predominant part of the community.
There are many other national origins represented, also, but these make up the
majority.

The Swedish immigrants settled to the north of the city in what is now Swede
Township in the 1880s and have the honor of the first postoffice in the region.
At that time the Northern Pacific was building into LaMoure County through
LaMoure and mail would be brought by horse or ox-driven vehicle from LaMoure.
As the NP progressed to Edgeley mail was brought in from there.  The North
Dakota Historical Society has record of the post office at Newburg, on the Eric
Bjur farm, with Bjur postmaster, being established in May of 1888.  It contined
in service until 1892 when the postoffice at Kulm was established.  The Robert
Lundgren farm now occupies the hilltop where Newburg Postoffice stood.  Everyone
passing the location can see the farm on the north side of high 13 about a half
mile east of 4-mile corner (4 miles north of Kulm).


[picture:  RAFTING CATTLE TO SUMMER PASTURE in Sweden about 1896 or 7.  Cattle
and family are carried on the raft with two boats pulling, taken in Omsele
province.  From the left are Helmer Johnson, herder, Hugo Ogren about 7-1/2 
years, his mother Maria and father Eric.  In the boat is Mrs. Teckla Davidson 
and Fannie Gran.]


In the 1957 Jubilee Book the story of how the city was named for Kulm, South
Russia, and Kulm, Germany is told.  This only whets the appetite for more
knowledge on the subject of why these people moved from Germany to Russia and
then to Dakota, and why the name of Kulm.

Kulm is not too unusual a name outside our United States.  In the course of
research on the matter it was found there are Kulm, Germany; Kulm in East
Prussia near the river Vistual (on some maps spelled Chulm, the Polish version);
Kulm, Switzerland; and Kulm, Austria.  There is also a Kulm Island in the Arctic
Sea.  It is very difficult to find maps that show Kulm in the area near the
Black Sea where German ancestors farmed and worked, or Bessarabia as the area
was known.  An old map carries the town name of Kulmscaia, probably a
Russianization of the name after the Germans left.


[picture:  IN THE DAKOTAS the first homes were made of clay brick or adobe.
This is the second clay house built by Christ Flegel and John Sperling in 1899.
That's Christ and John heading for town in the wagon.]


Kulm, in Bavaria, the part of Germany where our town name came from was the
location of a battle in 1813 on the 29th and 30th of August, when the French
Army was defeated by the Russian, Austrian and Prussian armies.  It is a town of
approximately 11,665.

The migrations leading to the naming of Kulm, South Russia, and subsequent
migration to Dakota, occurred in two great waves of Germans from the home
country.  The first was the Volga colonies, made up of 1000 professional people
desired by the Russian government and the balance drifters, dreamers and
indigent bankrupts that Germany would allow to leave.  The German government
heavily restricting movement to the Volga colonies.

Russia's second call for settlers was for the Black Sea Colonies in the area
between the Dnieper and Prut rivers.  They settled in what was then known as the
colonies of Groszliebental, Johannestal, Friedenstal, Sarata, Kulm and many
others in the great migrations from 1804 to 1817.  This area was known as
Bessarabia, and now is listed on maps as Moldavia, or Modavian SSR.

This area was ceded to Russia at the peace treaty of Jassy in 1792 by Turkey,
and included all territory from Astrakhan to the river Dneiper.  The Taters
in the area refused to bow to Russian authority so the Germans were brought
into the region as Tater-tamers.

These German peoples were hand-picked farmers, greatly desired by the Russians
to settle the region.  Their reasons for leaving Germany were many, including
the promise of free land, freedom from military service, better living
conditions and other concessions.  They were promised tax exemption for ten
years, cash grants to be repaid in 20 years, and equal treatment with native
Russians.  Each family received thirty Deszjatine of land.  The promise was of a
better life than in the years of wars, drought and depression in Germany.

Historians report that the Germans were deceived from the minute they set foot
on Russian soil.  They found conditions very hard, no trees, no fuel, and the
Germans weren't allowed to teach, so the second generation of settlers grew up
illiterate and in virtual slavery.  Debts piled up to the crown.  It was very
hard.

But these people made progress, and named one of their towns Kulm, taught at
home and made such progress in education that the Russian government made them
teach Russian in 1880.  They also made financial progress and the native
population looked on the German colonies with jealousy.

In 1848 Alexander II, Czar, made a general overhaul of Russian laws, and the
Germans found themselves subject to universal military service.  All other
promises the Russians had made to the colonists were long - past broken, but
they withstood the setbacks and prospered.  This last change broke the spirit of
the colonists and they began migrating to other countries, particularly the
United States.  Those who stayed behind were sent to Siberia as traitors, put in
camps, disowned, jailed and generally forsaken.  The Germans who had settled one
of the richest grain growing areas in Russia, and secured it against the Turks,
were displaced.  Many of them found homes in South central North Dakota, central
and western South Dakota, and northern central Nebraska.

Kulm is on the northeastern edge of this great Russia-German settlement.

And, so..when the Soo Line advanced their right-of-way to this spot in 1892, it
is almost unthinkable that our city would have been named anything other than
Kulm.

----------

The postoffice was established at Kulm in January of 1893 with Fred Buechler
postmaster, and operator of the store in which it was housed.  The postoffice
was discontinued October 11, 1895, and mail was sent to LaMoure.  In May of
1902, on the eleventh, the postoffice was re-established with George Finch the
postmaster.  Kulm was the terminus of this branch of the Soo Line until 1898
when it was extended westward to Bismarck, Pollack, and many other German
communities.  Kulm was incorporated as a city in 1906.

----------

Many tongues are spoken in Kulm, and around, to this day, mainly Swedish and the
local German.  The Swedish maintain close ties with their mother country and it
isn't unusual when a party from Kulm and Swede township travel extensively in
Sweden visiting many friends and relatives there.

The German people speak the South Russian version of German.  This is a
predominantly German language with lots of Russian, Romanian, and Turkish words
incorporated into it.  Since the technical revolution in America, with hundreds
of new machines and methods, these words have been incorporated, or the English
word used in the midst of the sentence with little or no pause, and few thoughts
that it is used.  Anyone with an ear for languages can listen in a local cafe or
public place and pick out enough words to get a general idea of the line of
conversation.


[picture:  DRAGGING IN 1916, behind the horses is a member of the Albert
Nelson family.]

----------

Life in the 1800s wasn't easy in Sweden, South Russia, Germany, or Norway, or
any other of the countries that our settlers came from.  Most of the pioneers
farmed the hard way, tilling with oxen or horses and a trip to town meant long
hours on driving, a trip of several days in many cases.

David Mueller, Jr., recalls that his parents' family lived in a frame barn while
they built the house of homemade sun dried clay bricks and his father and uncle
running a horse power threshing machine hand fed by two men.  Mr. Mueller was
born in 1898.

Later his older brother and he pitched the straw from under the straw-carrier of
the old type threshing machines.  This job was known as straw bucking.  He
recalls how their father hauled grain in sacks to the elevator, as the elevators
then, were not equipped with wagon dumping devices, and when his sister, brother
and himself herded cattle walking barefooted, for at that time there was open
range and only a few fences.

The balance of Mr. Mueller's story has some interesting pioneer points:

In 1905 Mr. David Mueller Sr., sold out his entire farming interest to a party
now unknown and started operating the farm of his Mother-in-law until 1907,
while at Streeter four of the Mueller children were baptized at one baptismal
ceremony in their home.  Pastors in those days had to travel long distances by
horse and buggy to serve their members.  Occasional church services were held in
the pioneer home; also school was kept in [the] home.

Three children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Mueller while living at Streeter,
Gotthilf, Lydia and Edwin.

During the year 1907 the family moved back to Kulm and lived at Grandmother
Neumann's place for three months and David Jr's. father and some of his friends
decided to go south to evade the severe winters.  Through the aid of a land
agent by the name of Selmer, they traveled to Wichita Falls, Texas and located
land, making a down payment of two hundred dollars each; however, because of
railroad cars not arriving in time to move them and also they found that there
were no churches of their faith in this far away land, the party then abandoned
the plan, losing their money and land holdings.  Later rich oil wells were found
on this land in Texas.  They then decided to farm near Kulm.  Their father
bought the Mack place one mile west of Kulm.  The farm is now known as the Edwin
Mueller farm, bought from Gottlieb Dobler who operated a general merchandise
store at the time in the building that is now the Pool Hall owned by Albert
Ernst.


[picture:  SEATED ON HIS FATHER'S LAP is David Mueller Jr.  Picture taken in
1896.]


During the fall of 1907 the family moved out to this farm.  Their father, being
a blacksmith, had his tools in an unfinished shop and called his boys out in the
middle of the night in January of 1908 to help him nail up the west wall of the
building for a snow storm had started.  They worked for three hours to finish
the job.  David Jr. holding the lantern, his brother Otto handing the boards and
their father nailing them in place.  The second day after this blizzard, while
walking to school, the boys found frozen cattle along the road.  The cattle had
strayed from a farm owned by a man named Reichenstein, then living on the place
where Connie Kinzler's son now lives.

During 1911 a son, Albert was born to Mr. and Mrs. Mueller, Sr., and the same
year their daughter Lydia passed away.

In the years of 1912 and 1913 he recalls severe hail storms destroying their
crops completely of eight hundred acres.  As no insurance was carried, their
father was forced to buy seed both years from farmers living many miles south,
by the name of Adam Lemke and Gottfried Henneberg.

During the spring of 1918, David Jr. took sick and developed pleurisy.  Thru the
recommendation of Dr. Theurer, a new comer to Kulm, his father sent him to the
Lindlar Institute at Chicago.  After four months of staying at this hospital, he
fully recovered and, on returning home, went to work the same day he arrived.
The hospital bill and passenger fare for him and the doctor, both ways,
amounted to $750.00.  After arriving home, he went to work unloading grain from
the header box.  David Jr. has enjoyed comparatively good health since and often
times claims jokingly that the first complicate machine he ever learned to
handle was a pitch fork, and he still can handle it quite well in spite of all
the modern improvements added.

In 1924, Mr. and Mrs. Mueller's son Albert passed away.

In 1928 Mr. and Mrs. Mueller Sr. retired from farming and moved to Kulm.

David Jr. started farming in 1931 and relates in the four years he farmed he
never used any harvesting machine as the drought destroyed all crops.  He went
to herding cattle then.

Mr. David Mueller, Sr. passed away December 22, 1950.  Mrs. David Mueller, Sr.
passed away February 25, 1952.

----------

But hard work and progress helped the farmers and merchants building the area.
Better machines and newer methods were developed and used.  Planting, haymaking
and harvesting became mechanized, crudely at first by present standards, but
ever better.

Along with the tractor came the automobile, not necessarily accepted right away
as these rules of the Anti-Automobile Society of 1909 will indicate.  The
society adopted the rules at conventions in various States.

1.  On discovering an approaching team, the automobilist must stop off-side and
cover his machine with a tarpaulin painted to correspond with the scenery.

2.  The speed limit on county roads this year will be secret, and the penalty
for violation will be $10 per mile for every mile an offender is caught going in
excess of it.

3.  In case an automobile makes a team run away, the penalty will be $50 for the
first mile, $100 for the second mile, $200 for the third mile, etc. that the
team runs; in addition to the usual damages.

4.  On approaching a corner where he cannot command a view of the road ahead,
the automobilist must stop not less than one hundred yards from the turn, toot
his horn, ring a bell, fire a revolver, haloo and send up three bombs at
internals of five minutes.

5.  Automobiles must be seasonably painted-that is, so they will merge with the
pastoral ensemble, and not be startling.  They must be green in spring, golden
in summer, red in autumn, and white in winter.

6.  Automobiles running on the county roads at night must send up a red rocket
every mile, and wait for the road to clear.  They may then proceed carefully,
blowing their horns and shooting roman candles.

7.  All members of the Society will give up Sunday to chasing automobiles,
shooting and shouting at them, making arrests, and otherwise discouraging
country touring on that day.

8.  In case a horse will not pass an automobile, not withstanding the scenic
tarpaulin, the automobilist will take the machine apart as rapidly as possible
and conceal the parts in the grass.

9.  In case an automobile approaches a farmer's house when the roads are dusty,
it will slow down to one mile an hour, and the chauffeur will lay the dust in
front of the house with a hand sprinkler worked over the dashboard.


[picture:  STEAM THRESHING-In 1897 John Ogren.  Boy with fork unknown.  On water
tank is Erick Malm.  Boy standing is Emil Anderson.  John Telberg and Andrew
Malm on top of Separator.  Rest are G.H. Davidson, P.A. Davidson, Eric Anderson
and Junius Johnson.]

[picture:  AN EARLY-DAY GATHERING.  Left to right in front on ground, Dave
Sperling, drayman, Dave Schlenker, drayman, and Mike Aamon, clerk for G. Lange.
Kneeling are Fred Roedel and E. Knecht.  Back row, sitting, Curly Rott
(Gottfred, a bartender).  Standing, John Jenner, Willie Lange, Gottfried Grosz,
Albert Freitag, harness maker for G. Gackle, Christ Hieb with cap, Christ
Kleiber, Christ Barnum; Rosco Organ (boy with white shirt), Gottfred Sperling
(middle in center), Bill Byers (mustache-worked in mill), Hank Palm (father of
Dell Palm), J.D. Smith and his dog.]

----------

Crime in Kulm was not a problem of major proportions, but occasional visits from
professionals were made in years gone by, as now, as evidenced by the following
story from the July 23 issue of a 1925 Messenger.

About 1 o'clock Saturday morning, July 15, 1925 H.E. Gansz, the cashier of the
First State Bank of Kulm, and his wife, arrived home from a visit in Ellendale.
Mr. Gansz, accompanied by his wife, drove his car into the garage at the rear of
the bank when two men stepped out from behind the garage doors.

"The Yeggs," as the Kulm Messenger describes them, "then politely asked the
Ganszes to face the wall.  After severing the car's ignition wires, the bandits
locked the Ganszes in the garage and told them to remain there.  Mr. Gansz said
they were treated politely at all times.

"The bank robbers proceeded to pry open the west window on the south side of the
Bank, entered and began their attempts to blast the vault open.  Using 13
charges of nitro-glycerin and still failing to open the vault, they finally gave
up.  There was 46 hundred dollars in the vault and none of it was serious
damaged, but about five thousand dollars worth of damage was done to the vault
and bank interior and furnishings.

"While the 'Yeggs' were blasting away at the vault, their accomplices, standing
guard outside, bombarded the streets and business places with over 10 shots.  As
soon as the noise of the explosions was heard the street lamps were lit and
lights went on throughout the town.  The robbers immediately shot out the street
lamps and poured found after round of lead into the hotel and other buildings
with lights burning.

"Assistant cashier N.G. Isaak, who later served as an alderman in Kulm for 34
years, and Adolph Smith got into Isaak's automobile and drove around town
spreading the alarm of the robbery.  During the process Isaak was shot in the
leg and taken to a doctor for treatment.  J.A. Melicher, who was also spreading
the alarm, fired three shotgun blasts at one robber but missed and then had to
flee.  Carl Mauch and Harry Wolf sneaked to the roof of the David Hettich store
and fired three shots across the street into the bank but immediately drew
return fire.  Fortunately the sharp shooting thieves believed the pair was
located in Mary Rott's Cream Station two doors south of Hettichs and blasted
away at that building.

"Inside the bank, the safe crackers finally gave up and called their forces
together to calmly leave town.  Before doing so, however, they severed telephone
and telegraph lines leading from town, leaving Kulm without any communications.

"At about this time in history other communities in the State were also being
plagued by the post-harvest invasion of bank bandits.  The adjutant General's
Office was swamped with requests for army rifles to be supplied to the towns for
self defense.  The requests were denied.

"The month after the attempted robbery in Kulm the residents formed a vigilance
committee and had two men guard the city every night.

"After some disagreement, the city board, at the urging of mayor Billigmeier,
finally approved 24 hour light service so the town could be lighted at night."

----------

[picture:  MECHANIZED THRESHING on the Gust Woehl farm.]

[picture:  KULM'S FAMOUR BASEBALL TEAM IN 1929.  Fighting back an attack of an
ambitious Fredonia Nine, Kulm's Baseball Club won the title in the LaMoure
County Baseball League in 1929.  At the end of the regular season Kulm was tied
with Fredonia and Diesem for first place, which necessitated a play-off.
Fredonia defeated Diesem 6 to 5 in the first game and Kulm defeated Fredonia 8
to 6 in the final encounter.  Reading from left to right the players are: (rear
row) Otto Sperling, second base; Ed Hildebrand, center field; Ernest Gackle,
left field; Ted Kreis, catcher; Harold Hildebrand, third base; (front row)
Adolph Hauff, right field; Philip Young, pitcher; Raymond Riese, mascot; William
Wolff, manager and first base; Gust Hildebrand, short stop]


Fired have always been a problem in small towns, and almost every town has had
its major fire, along with many minor ones.

We have had several large fires over the years, but the biggest occurred on
November 2, of 1905 when 16 business places were destroyed or damaged.

They were the bank and Charles Pruetz residence, Gackle and Company, John Grosz
and Son Store, Snyder Drug, Byer Brothers Pool & Bowling, Millers Restaurant,
Gordon's Barber Shop, Union Hotel, Johnson Bakery, Mippolt Hotel and Home,
Peterson Meat Market, Johnson's Restaurant, Malin's Real Estate, and the Kulm
Messenger.  Most of the buildings were rebuilt in brick.

The fire was discovered at 6 p.m. in the interior of the second story of Gackle
and Company Hardware, origin unknown.  In two hours the west side of the street
of Kulm's Business block was leveled.  Had there been a wind the toll could have
been much greater.  This tremendous damage prompted the Kulm Messenger to begin
campaigning for a fire protection program.

Residents of Kulm were more than slightly upset when passengers of an East bound
train stopped off to watch the fire and looted the business places.  Tar and
feathers was recommended.

On August 20, 1925, the Kulm Messenger carried the story of another big blaze.
Three days earlier at 3 p.m. a fire was discovered between the Bernhard Gehring
Institution and Mrs. Bertha Koldun's Restaurant.  The city had chemical fire
engines at the time, but the fire was too far gone before it was noticed and
little could be done.  Some of the stock carried out of the buildings was not
carried far enough away and was destroyed after all.  The fire claimed Gehrings,
Kolduns, and the Farmers Store, and damaged Gackle Brothers and the Bank.  If
the latter two had been lost it is likely everything southwest of there in the
business district would have gone also.

A life was lost in a fire here on April 26, 1926.  That again scarred Kulm's
Business District.

The fire was believed to have started in Becker's Lunchroom and novelty Shop.
Mr. Becker was killed trying to salvage family possessions.  The fire also
destroyed Swanke's Barber Shop and the Star Care, and damaged Straub's Butcher
Shop.  The Fredonia Fire Department assisted in fighting the blaze.

Three more business places went up in smoke on September 17, 1948, and this
resulted in a new business block for the town.

About 4 a.m. that fateful day flames engulfed Peoples Store, Art's Grocery and
the Hildebrand Locker Plant.  This time the Edgeley Fire Department was on hand
to battle the blaze.


[picture: GYM STYLES have changed since 1912 too.  Darlene Hartman models a
modern gym ensemble and one of regulation type at Valley City State College in
1912.  The older model gives more freedom of movement when running and bending,
but present problems for the tumbler.]

[picture:  ANGIE DICKINSON]


Angie Dickinson was born in Kulm-1931, the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. L.H. Brown.
Mr. Brown was the publisher of The Kulm Messenger.

The family left North Dakota in 1942 when Angie was 11 years old.  Moving to
Burbank, California.

In December of 1948, when Angie was senior at Bellamarmine Jefferson High School
in Burbank, she won the Sixth Annual Bill of Rights Contest in that community,
and two years later her sister Janet did likewise.  Being the daughter of a
printer, Angie at first had visions of becoming a writer but gave this up after
winner her first beauty contest.

After finishing college she worked as a secretary for a Burbank Airplane Parts
Factory for 3-1/2 years.

In 1953 she entered the local Miss America Contest one day before the deadline
and took second.  In August of the same year she was one of five winners in a
beauty contest sponsored by the National Broadcasting Company and appeared in
several Tv variety shows.  She got her first bit part in a Warner Brothers Movie
in 1954 and gained television fame in the millionaire series in 1956.  Her
success then spiraled until she became one of the nation's top movie stars which
she still is today.

What does Hollywood say about a star being born in North Dakota?  The newspapers
said, "Angie Dickinson was born in Kulm in a calm," implying that both are
extreme rarities in this State.

Angie reportedly began using her famous legs at birth when she kicked the doctor
in the eye.

She married her second husband, composer Burt Bacharach in May of 1965.

REMINISCING. . .


Names:  CHRIST KRUEGER; HENRY NATHAN; JOHN BADER; EMMANUEL BADER; AARON
REICHINSTEIN; SAM SENE; JOHN POKKERT; BERNHART JOHNSON; CARL JOHNSON, (Dragman);
CARL OBERG, came in 1895 went out west 1903; PETER BJUR, 1886- 1903;
HAMERSTRAND, Homesteaded in what is now Roys Park in 1895; GRUNDBERG, 1895-;
JOHN BROWN, 1895-; ED FORSMAN, 1906-1937; PERR OLSON, 1886-;OSCAR OLSON, 1886-;
SIGNA OLSON, 1886-; CARL JOHNSON, 1886-; ERICK JOHNSON, 1886-; RUDOLPH JOHNSON,
1890-; OSCAR JOHNSON, 1890-; CHRIST GUTHMILLER, 1898-1905; DANIEL SPERLING,
lived in section 6 of Norden Township and left in 1905.

GOTTFRED MAUCH, born in 1863, came to this country in 1899 and farmed in Swede
Township.  He married Freda Recha.

WILLIAM ZIEMAN, born in Germany in 1838, came to Swede Township in 1905.  He
married Mille Aulbright.  His son, Art, took over the farm later.  This farm is
now the Roy Davidson home.

GOTTFRIED ZUNDEL, born in Russia in 1882, settled in Swede Township in 1900.  He
married Justine Hehr.


Do you remember how the thriving little metropolis of Kulm looked in 1892?  It
was the year the railroad was being built.

C.J. Zellmer owned a general merchandise store located where the Kulm State Bank
is at present.

Looking south on the same block was John Grosz & Sons general merchandise store
which handled groceries, clothing and shoes.  His store was originally in Dickey
and was moved to Kulm.

Next was George Gackle's 2 story building with living quarters upstairs, sold
furniture and hardware.

Next was Michael Hieb's general merchandise store.

Next was Lange's general merchandise where Palms and Kreins are right now.  It
was in front of this store the first cement sidewalk was built, by Mr. Gray,
which caused considerable discussion, the people being curious and skeptical,
hesitant about accepting its durability, clinging to the old board sidewalk as
being the better. J.M. Erbele had moved a building from Ashley and used it for
his general merchandise store.

On the east side of main street, near the railroad track, was a tailor ship run
by Mr. and Mrs. Mehrling.

Mr. Kuhn had a men's clothing store.

Next was Ernest Schneider's butcher shop and he also worked for J.B. Kessler who
had a general merchandise store.  He also owned a store in Edgeley.

Louis Guthmueller's store was on the corner.  John Jenner, who had come from
Tripp, S. Dak., worked for him and was harness maker with J.A. Melicher.

Across the street was Buechler's store.

Christ Hieb has a 5 and 10 cent store which was later sold to Linder & Anderson
and was converted into a general merchandise store.

Jake Roedel was a mason and built several buildings, one of them being Dr.
Brankle's office, the front being of natural stone.

John Reimen was one of the early photographers.

J.A.T. Bjornson had the Drug Store.

Smith one of the first butcher shops.

Jake and Inez Byers also had a drug store which later burned.

Mr. A. Miller was proprietor of the first hotel, a 2 story building.

Mrs. John Hillius ran a restaurant and John Hillius the Stage and Mail Line
between Edgeley and Kulm, taking passengers and mail everyday.

The Baptist Church was on the corner where the City Hall is at present.  It was
bought by the Evangelical Congregation and moved to its present location.

The First Congregational Church was sold and moved to Wm. Messing which he
remodeled into his home.  A new church was built which was later torn down and
later the present building was built.

The Congregation of the Reform Church bought the little 2 room school house and
was later moved to the corner where the Luck home is at present.

The town would not be complete without the busy saloons serving the glasses of
cold beer.  One was owned by Christoph Rott.  One could see the well dressed
bartender, Hank Palm with his vest and a big chain hung across and his derby
hat.

East of Gackle's elevator was St. Croix & Salzer Lumber Yard run by Peter
Burfenning, owned by Sawyer & Arnold.

Kulm State Bank was one of the first banks with S. Pruetz president.

LaMoure County Bank was started by Olaf Haukins, and was the place where Christ
Hieb learned his banking experience.

Emil Anderson was the first undertaker.  At that time there was no state law
compelling one to have an embalmer's license.  Later the law was passed making
it compulsory, so Emil received his education and license from Grand Forks, N.
Dak.

There were livery barns where people could get their horses shod and keep their
horses in the barns while buying their groceries.  Many came from long distances
and would spend the night in the stores, sitting around the stoves, and eating
their lunches, and drinking cider bought from the storekeeper.  Many brought
their grain in sacks to be sold and money used for their groceries and clothing.
Nick Crudens livery barn was on the east side.  Fred and Dave Brost and Mike ran
a Livery Stable.  They had an upstairs to their building with living quarters.
Sam Ruud had one of the first livery stables.

John Hiller bought Dave Krause's blacksmith and machine shop.

Dollar & Railson were behind Gackle's store.  Railson's home where Albert
Buerkle lives.

Victor Lane, originally from England, owned and operated the Dray Line and a
hotel.  They had 2 children, Harriet and Douglas.

Otto Isaak, cashier of the First State Bank, presently living in Fargo.

Martin Erbele, cashier of the First State Bank, Otto Groh ran the first light
plant in 1911 in the old creamery.

Oscar Anderson was in partnership with his brother Emil in a grocery and dry
goods store.  Later, moved to Fergus Falls, Minn., died in 1959.

Fred Rott worked for the Soo Line, was a cream buyer and was city Mayor.

John Hiller owned a garage and blacksmith shop.  Later moved to Lodi, Calif.

John Erickson, Mayor of Kulm, and foreman on the Soo Line.

Bernard Steen, manager of the Farmers Elevator.

Arthur Bjur, worked at the Mill.

Jacob Ruff sold his share in the Kulm Mill to Reinhold Beich and moved to
Sacramento, Calif.

Otto Becker owned a restaurant which burned in 1926 where he lost his life.

John & Curly Rott had a Dray Line in 1904.

John A. Stolt was manager of the Farmers Elevator from about 1905 to 1916.  He
married Hilda Norelieus.

Louis Schmidt operated the Salzer Lumber yard.  He married Rebecca Kurtz.

Charles Freter, farmer, was born in Germany in 1874 and settled in Kulm 1904.

William Kramlich, farmer, was born in Russia in 1880 and settled in LaMoure
County in 1902.  He was married to Dora Sticker.  To this union 9 children were
born.  They are Tillie Krieger, Billings, Mont., Leona Gumke, Kremlin, Mont.,
Cecelia Gebbart, Lehr, N.D., Otelia, Mrs. Otto Rutschke, Kulm, Emma Hauser,
Kremlin, Mont., Ernest, who passed away in August 1966, Otto living in Arthur,
N.D., and Reinhold, owner of the Kramlich Locker in Kulm.

Joseph Rost was born in Wisconsin in 1868 and came to Kulm in 1902.  He was
married to Jane Riley.

Dave Keller, a farmer in Pomona View Township, was born in 1876 and settled in
LaMoure County in 1905.  He was married to Amelia Kack.

C. Flegel was manager of the Ideal Land Company.

O.A. Hoskins was editor of the Kulm Messenger in 1913.

Emanuel Siegler was a photographer in 1913.  Enlarging and frame work was his
speciality.

M.F. Brauer and George Schneider were in partnership in the Blacksmith,
horseshoers, and boiler repairs.  They also sold Acme Harvesting machinery, farm
implements and wagon in 1913.  Schneider was also city policeman.

Charles Lindor was in partnership with Christ Hieb and Emil Anderson in a
hardware and grocery store, which was located on the corner where the Farmers
Union building now stands.  From Kulm, he moved to Duluth, Minn., where he
passed away.

Ernest Schneider, brother of George, had a butcher shop located where the
present Kulm Cafe now stands.  The railroad was built west of Kulm in 1898, at
that time.  He was also a veterinarian.  He was married to Christina Mogck.

Kulm native, the Reverend Arthur Patzer, received national recognition in 1956
and 1957 when his talks on television, Rock and Roll music, and juke box
religion was heard throughout the world.  Vice President Nixon personally
congratulated him in 1957 for his stand on temperance.  Pastor Patzer is still
fighting for his cause on the east coast.

On August 22, 1957, Air Force Major David Simons landed 40 miles southeast of
here from a high altitude balloon flight begun in Minnesota two days earlier.

Lawrence Welk got his start playing for wedding dances in this area and many of
the folks remember dancing to his music.  Some even recall helping him out
financially when he started out.  His first paid dance was in Ipswich, S.D., and
he has not been without a contract since 1938.  He frequently visits his home
and family at Strasburg.

On November 22, 1956, Mr. and Mrs. Jonas Erickson, formerly of Kulm, observed
their 60th wedding anniversary in British Columbia, Canada.  Their family
consisted then of 10 children, 32 grandchildren and 12 great grandchildren for a
total of 56.  In those 60 years there had not been one death or one divorce
among any of the 56 members.

Kulm observed its 65th anniversary on June 24 and 25 of 1957 because the 50th
anniversary occurred during the war and many oldtimers feared they would not be
around to see the 75th anniversary.  Seven thousand people poured into our
little community.  The speaker was Claude Ebling, Soo Line Agricultural Agent
and Farm Philosopher.

On October 20, 1955, city engineer Paul Barnes of Valley City surveyed Kulm for
street paving.  The project was completed in 1956.

The first railroad car of corn was shipped east for sale by the Gackle Grain
Company on January 10, 1924.  It brought Dan Hildebrand 51 cents a bushel.

Incidentally, the box car shortage we hear so much about now was already a
problem in Kulm in 1907.  Being located in the corner of three counties, the
Kulm farming county was a great wheat center and the elevators had trouble
getting enough box cars to ship their grain out.

Kulm businessman and State Senator J.B. Sharpe was instrumental in organizing
the senate in the state legislature.

Curb parking was begun in Kulm, August 22, 1925, because the Saturday night
crowds were presenting a parking problem when everyone parked in the middle of
the street.

The first school was started in 1893 and the first high school graduating class
was in 1927 when two people graduated.  On January 3, 1924, at 4 a.m. sparks
from the chimney ignited the shingles on the school and it burned down in one
and a half hours.  Classes were held in the City Auditorium until a new school
was completed later that same year.

A new elementary school was constructed in 1961 and the school plus, remodeling
of the high school, cost 290 thousand dollars.

The American Legion provided a lighted athletic field in July of 1958, making
Kulm one of the smallest towns in the state with a lighted field.  In the first
contest on the field, Kulm's baseball team defeated Edgeley 3 to 1.

Another great honor came to Kulm when Coach Glen Robinson's basketball team won
the state tournament in March of 1958.  The present superintendent of schools,
Owen Wallace, was the assistant coach.  The team members were: Phil Mueller and
Gilbert Schott as co-captains, Gerry Herman, Ron Sandau, Tom Herman, Delano
Herrmann, Arnie Heck, Duane Theilen, Bobby Hieb, and Adolph Puhlman.  Coach
Robinson served his team breakfast in bed for accomplishing the feat.

In the first game, Kulm downed Underwood 50-49 and committed only seven fouls.
There was a roaring welcome for the boys when they returned home that Sunday
morning.

Kulm had made a trip to the state, it's first, the previous year, but did not
fare very well that time.

Basketball was begun here in 1908 and the first team consisted of Robert Lange,
Christ Dobler, Eddie Guthmueller, John Stolt and Russel Burfenning.

The big blizzard in March of last year was by no means the worst Kulm has ever
seen.  In January of 1907, the folks were getting two blizzards a week
accompanied by extreme cold weather.  This lasted for four weeks.  The fuel
shortage was becoming critical but trains could not travel often enough to
alleviate the situation.  One passenger train was snowed in near Fredonia for
six days.  No loss of life was reported, however.

The Kulm Health Center was formed in 1951 but was without the services of a
doctor for about a year once.  The Governor and Congressmen were called upon for
help until Dr. Richard Steidl came in July of 1954.

Perhaps the best known doctor here was F.W. Fergusson who served the community
from 1921 until he retired in 1950 and moved to Lodi, California.  He died there
in September of 1953 at the age of 61.  Twenty-five hundred people were present
to honor him on Dr. Fergusson Day here July 28, 1946, marking his 25th
anniversary here.

Former Mayor W.M. Gackle was the first boy born here and former postmaster Oscar
Lange was the second boy born in Kulm.


BIRTHS-

Following is a list of births from back issues of The Kulm Messenger as
nearly as available copies will permit.  Papers were scanned very carefully
week by week from the first copies available at the historical society in
Bismarck, and in the office locally, in an effort to make this a reasonably
complete reference book for the town.  In most cases, early papers didn't
give the infant's name, but perhaps families can make educated guesses from
dates and parents names.  [Arranged in date sequence where not sequential]

MARRIAGES-

This list of weddings in Kulm, and concerning those known in Kulm, have been
compiled from a week-by-week search of back issues of the THE KULM MESSENGER.
[Arranged in date sequence where not sequential]



1898

Alexander BURNET and Miss Jeanette THAIN were married on February 11.

Molly REIMAN, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Reiman, Kulm, married Laurence
DENGERUD of Wilmar, Minn. on July 16.


1905

Mr. John H. MONTAI of Kulm and Mrs. Julia WOLFER of Valley City were married
July 24.

Mr. Fred F. HIXON and Eunice D. CLINE both of Kulm, N.D., were married August 1,
1905.

Mr. Gottfried JOHANSON and Mrs. Anna SIMONSON were married August 20.  They both
are from Forman.

Otto GROH and Miss Alice Grace SEMPEL, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Warren Sempel,
were married October 5, 1905.

On November 18, Jos. C. HOFMEISTER, of Maple Lake, Minn., was married to Miss
Pearl SMITH, of Kulm, at Edgeley.  Miss Smith is the daughter of Mr. and Mrs.
B.F. Smith.

Miss Pearl MOREY and John JOHNSON were married Thanksgiving evening.  Miss Morey
is the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. A.P. Morey.

December 10, John EKLUND and Miss Hilma HELGREN were united in marriage.


1906

Wm. HOEGER and Miss Linda MALLENHAUER were united in marriage, March 7, 1906.

Fred LARES and Louisa DAGERT, both of Kulm, were married August 27, 1906.

On September 2, 1906, Lena HIEB, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Michael Hieb and A.F.
LEHR, of Gackle, were united in marriage.

Myron NOYES and Miss Laura SMITH were married September 11, 1906.  Miss Smith is
the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. J. Smith.

Mr. Ardon D. GORDON, of Kulm, and Miss Myra Dott PERKINS, of Villard, Minn.,
were united in marriage on October 30.

Carl K. DAHL and Miss Lelia PREBLE were united in marriage November 13, 1906.

December 19, 1906, David HASZ and Miss Lydia HENNEBERG were married.

Adolf GROSZ, son of Mr. and Mrs. Daniel Grosz, and Miss Lydia HANNELD were
married December 19, 1906.

Gackle and Ruth Hieb are trying out the seats of the current model auto.]


[picture:  ONE DAY DELIVERY OF BINDERS AND HEADERS IN 1910.  George Gackle in
front row, with vest, (seldom seen without a vest or his customary wearing
apparel).  Sitting on Headers are-Peter Billigmeier, Otto Gackle, 10 years of
age and Ernest Gackle, 7 years of age.  A close look one may find among the
group, one's grandfather or father.]


DEATHS-

In an effort to complete the history of Kulm and its people, a directory of
those who served the city and region, for short or long periods of time and who
have gone on, leaving the area to the next generation, have been listed, in
conjunction with the two preceding lists.  A search of these lists will allow
almost any family's tree to be traced, in and about the city. [Arranged in date
sequence where not sequential]


1902

Andrew GOEHNER died July 24.


1904

Mrs. Selma MANTEI died January 14.  She was born in Russia.


1905

Gottlieb GRUNEICH, of Kulm, died August 28, 1905.

Mrs. Geo. RENSCHELLER, of Kulm, died October 30, 1905.

Nicolai STEHR, father of Mrs. G. Lange, died December 28, 1905.  He was born in
Russia in the year of 1813.


1906

Mrs. H.E. PUTNAM died January 15, 1906.  Mrs. Putnam was born in Iowa in 1873.

George Patrick BRADY passed away May 1, 1906.  Mr. Brady was born in LeSeuer
County, Minn., November 1883.

Erick BJUR, Sr. died October 21, 1906.

Benjamin F. SMITH died March 18, 1906.  He was born at Orland, Indiana, June 10,
1854.


1907

James GALBRAITH died November 15, 1907.  He was born at Endeavor, Wisc., on
October 28, 1842.

The 18 month old twin son of Mr. and Mrs. Jacob GOHNER died December 1, 1907.

Walter Macomber BURFENING, son of Mr. and Mrs. P.C. Burfening, died December 31,
1907.  He was born January 4, 1895.


1908

Andreas HAUF died January 28, 1908 at the age of 84 years old.

Mrs. John (Eva) BIEG died last week.  She was the daughter of Johann Schlecht
and was 44 years old.

Gottfried KLAUDT died March 13, 1908.  Mr. Klaudt was born at Paris, South
Russia on January 27, 1877.

Mrs. Ludvig JOHNSON passed away March 18, 1908.  Anna Carolina Hallgren was born
in Sweden and was 34 years old at the time of her death.

The three and one-half year old daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John FLEGEL died May 8,
1908 of scarlet fever.

Infant daughter of Mr. and Mrs. M.F. BRAUER died July 2, 1908.

The five month old son of W.J. MENSING died July 31, 1908.

Erick OGREN died August 11, 1908.  He was born in Sweden on April 18, 1832.

John F. ENGBERG died October 26, 1908.  He was born at Kiron, Iowa on December
13, 1869.


1909

Mrs. Margaret TJERNLUND died January 7, 1909.  She was born in Sweden on
November 3, 1830.

Mrs. Fred BOSSERT, from Piedmont, Mo., died February 10, 1909.

Phillip ENTZMINGER died February 22, 1909.  He was about 35 years old.

Irene Marie VOGEL, daughter of Rev. and Mrs. A.H. Vogel, died February 28, 1909.
She was born January 15, 1902 at Fall Creek, Ill.

Mrs. Peter GRAMITA died March 13, 1909.

Anton BOWMAN died March 11, 1909.  He was born in Jorn, Sweden, March 5, 1848.

The 7 week old daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Jacob GOEHNER died about March 28, 1909.

Mrs. A.B. MALIN died April 7, 1909.  She was born in Freemont, Ohio on March 13,
1868.

Mrs. Christian PRUETZ died April 27, 1909. She was born in Brunn, Germany on
June 24, 1831.

Mrs. John BADER died May 17, 1909.  She was born in New [Neu] Posttal,
Bessarabia, Russia, October 17, 1872.

P.J. BRADY died June 15, 1909, born in Ireland, January 14, 1844.

Infant son of Mrs. John HERRMANN died August 17, 1909.

Mrs. Caroline HEINE died September 27, 1909.  She was 77 years of age.

Fred BOSSART died October 20, 1909.  He was 75 years old.

Mrs. C. SCHLENKER died November 6, 1909.

Mrs. Elmer TJERNLUND died about January 9, 1910.  She was 20 years of age.

Eight month old baby of Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried MUELLER died about February 12,
1910

Mrs. HEER died February 12, 1910.  She was 47 years of age.

Two month old son of Mr. and Mrs. Andrew LAY died about February 13, 1910.

Andreas SHAEDLER died February 18, 1910.  He was 24 years old.

Miss Hulda ARVIDSON died March 22, 1910.  She was 15 years of age.

Mrs. F.G. HILDEBRAND (Louise Radke) died April 1, 1910.  She was born at Kulm,
South Russia on December 16, 1867.

Marie TOBLER died May 30, 1910.  She was one and one-half years old.

Wilhelm KLETTKE died about June 20, 1910.

The 1 year old daughter of Gottlieb DUERR died June 29, 1910.

Mrs. Gottlieb HAUFF died June 27, 1910.  She was 65 years old.

Alice Esther THIELE died July 17, 1910.  She was 2 years old.

Mrs. Maria Sophia Nelson OGREN died August 3, 1910.  She was born in Amsele, of
Waterbotten Lan, Sweden.  She was 51 years old.

Theodore ROEDEL died October 15, 1910.  He was 9 months old.

John KROLL died October 23, 1910.  He was 73 years old.

Carolina HANNAMON died October 30, 1910.

Hjelmer STROM died November 27, 1910.  She was born October 2, 1879, at

Mrs. Daniel GROSZ, Jr. died November 22, 1910.  She was born July 17, 1871, in
